# Claims matrix: recurring quantitative claims

Generated 2026-10-15 by a scratchpad extractor over the Abstract, Ch. 1, §3.5, Ch. 4, Ch. 5, Ch. 8, Ch. 9 and Ch. 10 (comments stripped; sentences split heuristically; a sentence may appear under several topics). 417 trigger sentences.

## Standard-form norm and degree

| # | Location | Sentence |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:19` | What remains is intrinsic to the Hamiltonian: each derivative is eliminated through the dense Chebyshev differentiation matrix, whose norm grows as $N^2$, so for a second-order equation with constant coefficients the Hamiltonian's norm grows as $N^8$ and the number of applications as $N^4$, however it is encoded. |
| 2 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | All five are resource estimates, each computed from a subnormalisation and the classically computed gap; the standard form's degrees lie far beyond the simulation budget. |
| 3 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:169` | The Hamiltonian's norm falls from $\Theta(N^{4k})$ for an order-$k$ equation with constant coefficients to $\Theta(N^2)$, and the residual compiles to a single \gls{lcu} with $\mathcal{O}(\log n)$ ancilla qubits, $\lceil\log_2(n+4)\rceil+8$ for the oscillators' equation block. |
| 4 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:169` | On a regular problem the gap is proved bounded below independently of $N$ (\Cref{thm:flat-gap}), and the degree grows as $\tilde\Theta(N)$, where for constant coefficients the standard form's grows as $\tilde\Theta(N^{2k})$ even under the best exact encoding. |
| 5 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:171` | On singular problems, whose leading coefficient vanishes somewhere on $[-1,1]$, an a priori block rescaling restores the gap without ancilla, lengthening each query by a factor that the equation fixes, and the degree then grows as about $N^2$; on Legendre's equation the standard form needs the smaller degree at the ideal bound. |
| 6 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:171` | On two axes the rescaled gap still falls with $N$, and on the heat equation at four qubits per axis the standard form needs the lower degree. |
| 7 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:176` | It exists to make the comparison fair: for constant coefficients it holds the encoder's cost to a constant, so whatever growth remains in the standard form's degree belongs to its Hamiltonian. |
| 8 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:181` | The floor on the standard form's degree is shown for polynomial filters of the walk, and since Lin and Tong's optimality result for ground-state preparation assumes access to $H$ rather than to its residual \cite{lintong2020groundstate}, whether it binds every algorithm is left open. |
| 9 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:172` | $\G$ is strictly upper triangular, dense and nilpotent, and its norm grows as $N^2$: $\\|\G\\|_2/N^2$ converges to $1/(2j_{-3/4,1})\approx0.472$, with $j_{-3/4,1}$ the first positive zero of the Bessel function $J_{-3/4}$, and $\\|\G^k\\|_2/N^{2k}$ to a positive constant for every $k$. |

## Descriptor norm, degree and gap

| # | Location | Sentence |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:21` | For linear problems on one axis with polynomial coefficients, analytic data and a leading coefficient that never vanishes, we prove that the spectral gap stays bounded away from zero as the resolution grows, so the number of applications grows about linearly in $N$; for the oscillator it falls to $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$, although on constant-coefficient problems each application can cost more gates. |
| 2 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:21` | On one axis a rescaling fixed in advance restores it without extra qubits, and the number of applications then grows about as $N^2$ when the leading coefficient vanishes. |
| 3 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:25` | The descriptor form moves the conditioning of differentiation from the norm into the gap, which can be secured on one axis but not yet on two. |
| 4 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | The descriptor form developed in this thesis is such a Hamiltonian, and on the same problem its filter needs a degree of $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$ as built and $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{ideal_degree}$ at its own ideal bound. |
| 5 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:169` | The Hamiltonian's norm falls from $\Theta(N^{4k})$ for an order-$k$ equation with constant coefficients to $\Theta(N^2)$, and the residual compiles to a single \gls{lcu} with $\mathcal{O}(\log n)$ ancilla qubits, $\lceil\log_2(n+4)\rceil+8$ for the oscillators' equation block. |
| 6 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:169` | On a regular problem the gap is proved bounded below independently of $N$ (\Cref{thm:flat-gap}), and the degree grows as $\tilde\Theta(N)$, where for constant coefficients the standard form's grows as $\tilde\Theta(N^{2k})$ even under the best exact encoding. |
| 7 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:171` | On singular problems, whose leading coefficient vanishes somewhere on $[-1,1]$, an a priori block rescaling restores the gap without ancilla, lengthening each query by a factor that the equation fixes, and the degree then grows as about $N^2$; on Legendre's equation the standard form needs the smaller degree at the ideal bound. |
| 8 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:171` | On two axes the rescaled gap still falls with $N$, and on the heat equation at four qubits per axis the standard form needs the lower degree. |
| 9 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:171` | Carrying the intermediate derivatives instead of eliminating them therefore makes the residual banded and keeps its kernel, lowering the Hamiltonian's norm from $\Theta(N^8)$ to $\Theta(N^2)$ for a second-order equation; the price is a block register and a gap that the derivative blocks erode, which an a priori rescaling restores on one axis without ancilla (\Cref{sec:descriptor,sec:results-scaling,sec:results-gap}). |

| 11 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:395` | \begin{proposition}[Cost of the structured encoding] \label{prop:standard-cost} The factorisation of \Cref{prop:G-factorisation} compiles to an exact block encoding of $\G$ on $n$ system qubits with \begin{equation} \alpha_{\G} = N(N-1), \qquad \frac{\alpha_{\G}}{\\|\G\\|_2} \longrightarrow 2j_{-3/4,1} \approx 2.117, \qquad O(n^2)\ \text{gates}, \qquad n + 4\ \text{ancilla} , \label{eq:standard-cost} \end{equation} of which $n-1$ ancilla are the selector of the $N/2$-term combination $\Uodd$. \end{proposition} The limit is that of $\\|\G\\|_2/N^2$ (\Cref{sec:standard-operators}). |

| 13 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:112` | The mass form keeps the standard form's state, initial state and readout, so it adds no qubits and has no derivative block to rescale (\Cref{sec:descriptor-rescaling}). |

| 15 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:134` | \subsection{The Descriptor's Gap} \label{sec:descriptor-gap} |
| 16 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:139` | With $E$ the map of \Cref{thm:kernel-equivalence}, the descriptor's Rayleigh quotient on the states that satisfy the recurrences is \begin{equation} \frac{\\|R_{\mathrm{desc}}Ep\\|^2}{\\|Ep\\|^2} = \frac{\\|R_{\mathrm{std}}\,p\\|^2}{\sum_{m=0}^{k}\\|\G^{m}p\\|^2}, \label{eq:descriptor-quotient} \end{equation} because the recurrence and padding rows vanish on $Ep$ and the remaining rows act as the standard form's. |
| 17 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:148` | An equation on one axis whose $a_k$ has a zero on $[-1,1]$ is \emph{singular}. \end{definition} Of the benchmark problems, R1, R2 and R4b--d are regular, and R3 and R4a singular. \begin{theorem}[Single-axis gap] \label{thm:flat-gap} The gap of the descriptor residual of a regular problem, in the chain unscaled or block-rescaled at a fixed $\zeta$, or in the mass form for a first-order equation or system, is $\Theta(1)$ as $N\to\infty$. \end{theorem} The proof (\Cref{app:flat-gap}) reads each recurrence row as the defect between block $j+1$ and the derivative of block $j$, measured in the norm of the second-kind polynomials, integrates it, and closes by compactness, so it bounds the gap away from zero without giving the bound. |
| 18 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:155` | On R2a the unscaled gap is $\res[3]{build/R2a/descriptor/n10/unscaled}{gap}$ at both $n=3$ and $n=10$. |
| 19 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:155` | On the stiff equation $f''+5f'+400f=0$ (R2c, Wu et al.'s Fig.~3d), whose solution oscillates at an angular frequency near $20$, the gap is $\res[2,sci]{build/R2c/descriptor/n10/unscaled}{gap}$ at both $n=5$ and $n=10$, flat and small, and $w_f$ is $\res[2,sci]{build/R2c/descriptor/n10/unscaled}{field_weight}$, against $\res[2,sci]{build/R2a/descriptor/n10/unscaled}{field_weight}$ on R2a. |
| 20 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:159` | A polynomial of degree $N$ can concentrate within $\mathcal{O}(N^{-2})$ of an endpoint, where the Chebyshev points cluster, and each of its derivatives grows by a factor $\mathcal{O}(N^{2})$ there, the extremal behaviour of Markov's inequality \cite{borweinerdelyi1995}. |
| 21 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:159` | The measured gap falls as about $N^{-4}$: on Legendre's equation $(1-x^2)f''-2xf'+20f=0$ (R3a), whose leading coefficient vanishes at both ends, it is $\res[2,sci]{build/R3a/descriptor/n5/unscaled}{gap}$ at $n=5$ and $\res[2,sci]{build/R3a/descriptor/n10/unscaled}{gap}$ at $n=10$, and on $(x-1)f''-xf'+f=(x-1)^2$ (R4a, Wu et al.'s Fig.~5a), whose leading coefficient vanishes at $x=1$, $\res[2,sci]{build/R4a/descriptor/n5/unscaled}{gap}$ and $\res[2,sci]{build/R4a/descriptor/n10/unscaled}{gap}$. |

| 23 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:160` | On the heat equation $\partial_tf-\tfrac{1}{25}\partial_x^2f=0$ (R5b, Wu et al.'s Fig.~6d) the gap is $\res[2,sci]{build/R5b/descriptor/n4x4/unscaled}{gap}$ at $n=4$ per axis, the first resolution at which both forms' references are trusted, and $\res[2,sci]{build/R5b/descriptor/n5x5/unscaled}{gap}$ at $n=5$, where the standard form's is $\res[3]{build/R5b/standard/n4x4}{gap}$ and $\res[3]{build/R5b/standard/n5x5}{gap}$. \end{enumerate} The collapse is not an artefact of the lift, the padding, the arithmetic precision, the constraints, the basis or the classical reference: each was tested and ruled out, and a descriptor built in the ultraspherical basis collapses at the same rates (\Cref{app:descriptor-gap}). |

gap proved $\Theta(1)$ & the chain with the a priori $\zeta$ for $k\ge2$, the mass form for $k=1$; expect more gates per query at small $n$ \\ Singular, simple zero (R3, R4a) & degree about $\tilde\Theta(N^2)$; against $N^4$ where the zero is at one end (R4a), none at the ideal bound where it is at both (R3) & $\zeta=N/2$; judge at the ideal bound, since as built the gain on R3 is the encoder's \\ Singular, double zero & none claimed: balancing needs a norm above the standard form's own & substitute $f=\sqrt{1-x^2}\,v$ to leave simple zeros, as for R3c and R3d \\ Two axes, no mixed derivative (R5) & degree's exponent per axis below the standard form's; reversed on the heat equation at $n=4$ per axis; gap falls even when rescaled & $\zeta_a=N_a$; budget a state of four to eight blocks; compare at the ideal bound and expect no guaranteed gain \\ First-order systems (R7) & as on a regular problem: about $N$ against $N^2$ & the mass form, with no block register and a larger gap than a chain's; data as ratio rows; the coupling as matchings \\ Complex-valued linear equations & that of the real system after realification (R7 is $z'=-\mathrm{i}\pi z$ realified) & realify, at one more field-register qubit; native complex preparation is not built \\ Polynomial nonlinearity, lift converging (R8; R9 to $a/\nu=3$; R10 to $a/\nu=10$, and at $30$ on $12$ modes) & through the time axis only, from $n_t=4$, narrowing as the modes grow & the Carleman lift, mass form in time; raise $n_t$ with the truncation; measure the lift's error per order; never the doubled space, which projects \\ Past the lift's boundary (R9 at $a/\nu=10$; R10 at $a/\nu=30$ from $28$ modes) & none: the kernel stays trusted while the lift diverges & shorten the horizon or lower the strength; read convergence from the lift's error, never from trust \\ \bottomrule \end{tabularx} \end{table} |

| 119 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:145` | A filter of $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$ sequential walks of $\res{query/stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{gates}$ two-qubit gates each lies far beyond the depth that uncorrected processors execute reliably \cite{preskill2018nisq}, which is why noisy hardware is out of scope. |

| 20 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:145` | Every later degree, gap and subnormalisation claim is scoped by the class this singles out. \begin{definition}[Regular and singular problems] \label{def:regular} A linear problem on one axis, an equation of order $k$ or a first-order system $z'=\Acoup z$ with a constant coupling matrix, is \emph{regular} if its coefficients are polynomials and its leading coefficient ($a_k$, or the identity for a system) has no zero on $[-1,1]$; its source and its solution $f$ are analytic on $[-1,1]$; and its constraint rows read $f,\dots,f^{(k-1)}$ at fixed points, every $g\in C^k[-1,1]$ on which its equation and constraint rows vanish being a multiple of $f$. |
| 21 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:148` | An equation on one axis whose $a_k$ has a zero on $[-1,1]$ is \emph{singular}. \end{definition} Of the benchmark problems, R1, R2 and R4b--d are regular, and R3 and R4a singular. \begin{theorem}[Single-axis gap] \label{thm:flat-gap} The gap of the descriptor residual of a regular problem, in the chain unscaled or block-rescaled at a fixed $\zeta$, or in the mass form for a first-order equation or system, is $\Theta(1)$ as $N\to\infty$. \end{theorem} The proof (\Cref{app:flat-gap}) reads each recurrence row as the defect between block $j+1$ and the derivative of block $j$, measured in the norm of the second-kind polynomials, integrates it, and closes by compactness, so it bounds the gap away from zero without giving the bound. |
| 22 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:157` | The gap falls with $N$ in two cases. \begin{enumerate} \item \emph{A singular problem. |
| 23 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:159` | At an interior zero, where the resolution is $\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})$, Bernstein's inequality in place of Markov's gives $N^{-2}$ \cite{borweinerdelyi1995}; no benchmark problem has one, and \Cref{app:descriptor-gap} measures the rate on a controlled equation. \item \emph{Two axes. |

| 25 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:176` | For a singular one, $\zeta=N/2$, which raises the quotient of the concentrated states of \Cref{sec:descriptor-gap} to order one. |

## Tier reach (20 qubits, 2^20, 25-qubit exact verification)

| # | Location | Sentence |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:17` | A register of $n$ qubits holds $N=2^n$ amplitudes, so a quantum algorithm can store a discretised solution in exponentially few qubits. |
| 2 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:19` | For a critically damped oscillator on eight qubits, an exact encoding of the differentiation matrix lowers the estimated number of applications from $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax/encoder=published}{degree}$ with the published encoding to $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$, and no encoding of the same Hamiltonian can bring it below $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{ideal_degree}$. |
| 3 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:21` | Its norm grows only as $N^2$, and its circuit needs only $\mathcal{O}(\log n)$ auxiliary qubits. |
| 4 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:21` | For linear problems on one axis with polynomial coefficients, analytic data and a leading coefficient that never vanishes, we prove that the spectral gap stays bounded away from zero as the resolution grows, so the number of applications grows about linearly in $N$; for the oscillator it falls to $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$, although on constant-coefficient problems each application can cost more gates. |
| 5 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:21` | On one axis a rescaling fixed in advance restores it without extra qubits, and the number of applications then grows about as $N^2$ when the leading coefficient vanishes. |
| 6 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:25` | Circuits of up to $24$ qubits are verified against the operators they encode, and simulated circuits of up to $\res{stateprep/R4d/descriptor/n3/T1/filter=minimax}{n_qubits}$ qubits agree with an exact emulation to within $10^{-10}$. |
| 7 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:85` | A register of $n$ qubits holds a vector of $N=2^n$ amplitudes, and this exponential compression is the reason quantum algorithms for differential equations are proposed \cite{berry2014highorder,childsliu2020spectral,liu2021carleman}. |
| 8 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | Take the critically damped oscillator $f''+4f'+4f=0$, one of Wu et al.'s examples and here called R2a, at $n=8$ qubits, with the minimax edge filter, the polynomial of least degree that suppresses the excited states to $\varepsilon=\res[1,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{epsilon}$. |
| 9 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | With Wu et al.'s encoding of the differentiation matrix the filter's degree is $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax/encoder=published}{degree}$. |
| 10 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | An exact encoding of the same Hamiltonian brings it down to $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$, and no encoding of that Hamiltonian can do better than $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{ideal_degree}$, the degree at the ideal-encoding bound $\alpha=\\|R\\|$, where $R$ is the residual operator whose Gram matrix is $H$. |
| 11 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | The descriptor form developed in this thesis is such a Hamiltonian, and on the same problem its filter needs a degree of $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$ as built and $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{ideal_degree}$ at its own ideal bound. |
| 12 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | All five are resource estimates, each computed from a subnormalisation and the classically computed gap; the standard form's degrees lie far beyond the simulation budget. |

| 15 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:171` | On two axes the rescaled gap still falls with $N$, and on the heat equation at four qubits per axis the standard form needs the lower degree. |
| 16 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:175` | Their partial differential and nonlinear examples do not reproduce, and the hypotheses tested are recorded (\Cref{sec:standard}, \Cref{app:reproduction}). \item An exact factorisation of the differentiation matrix gives an encoding of $\G$ with $\mathcal{O}(n^2)$ gates and $n+4$ ancilla qubits, within a factor $\res[3]{derivative/n10}{structured_ratio}$ of its norm at $n=10$, against $\res[2,sci]{derivative/n10}{published_ratio}$ for Wu et al.'s approximate encoding. |
| 17 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:178` | Every Hamiltonian of at most $24$ qubits is verified whole against the operator assembled from the problem, every lift and coupling term by term at any size, and wherever both run, the simulated circuit equals the exact emulation within $10^{-10}$ before normalising on every run of the minimax filter (\Cref{sec:pipeline,sec:results-faithful}). \end{enumerate} |
| 18 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:181` | The evidence comes in three tiers, named wherever a number is quoted: circuit simulation (T1), which reaches $\res{stateprep/R4d/descriptor/n3/T1/filter=minimax}{n_qubits}$ qubits; exact emulation (T2), provably equal to the circuit's post-selected output, which reaches filter degrees of $2^{20}$; and resource estimates (T3) beyond that, a circuit too wide to simulate being reported as beyond the simulation budget and never as out of the algorithm's reach. |
| 19 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:328` | Its entries carry an error of order $N^{-5}$ (\Cref{prop:epsilon-G}), so it enters resource estimates only, inside the same stacked residual as the exact encoding below. |
| 20 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:389` | PREPARE (left) puts the offset register $\ell$ in a uniform superposition over the $N/2$ odd offsets, by an $X$ on its lowest qubit and Hadamards on the rest. |
| 21 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:389` | The block is $\Uodd/(N/2)$, so $\alpha=N/2$, on $n$ system and $n+2$ ancilla qubits.} \label{fig:uodd-select} \end{figure} |
| 22 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:395` | \begin{proposition}[Cost of the structured encoding] \label{prop:standard-cost} The factorisation of \Cref{prop:G-factorisation} compiles to an exact block encoding of $\G$ on $n$ system qubits with \begin{equation} \alpha_{\G} = N(N-1), \qquad \frac{\alpha_{\G}}{\\|\G\\|_2} \longrightarrow 2j_{-3/4,1} \approx 2.117, \qquad O(n^2)\ \text{gates}, \qquad n + 4\ \text{ancilla} , \label{eq:standard-cost} \end{equation} of which $n-1$ ancilla are the selector of the $N/2$-term combination $\Uodd$. \end{proposition} The limit is that of $\\|\G\\|_2/N^2$ (\Cref{sec:standard-operators}). |
| 23 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:410` | The $n-1$ selector qubits are the price of writing $\Uodd$ as one combination of its $N/2$ shifts, which \Cref{sec:descriptor-cost} sets against the descriptor's ancilla. |
| 24 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:438` | On R2a at $n=8$ the stacked residual's encoding has $\alpha_R=\res[3,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{alpha_R}$ against $\\|R_{\mathrm{std}}\\|_2=\res[3,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{norm_R}$, both computed exactly from the built circuit and the assembled residual, a ratio of $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{alpha_ratio}$, levelling off towards the constant looseness of the residual's dominant term $\G^2$ (\Cref{app:G-limits}), though no limit is proved for the whole residual. |
| 25 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:438` | Since the degree is linear in $\alpha_R$, this ratio is the whole of the encoder's cost in the degree; in $\alpha_H=\alpha_R^2$ it is squared, $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{hamiltonian_ratio}$ at $n=8$. |
| 26 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:438` | With Wu et al.'s encoding the same ratio is $\res[2,sci]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/standard/n7/T3/filter=minimax/encoder=published}{alpha_ratio}$ at $n=7$ and $\res[2,sci]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax/encoder=published}{alpha_ratio}$ at $n=8$: it grows as $N^{3k}$, a factor $N^3$ for each of the $k$ factors of $\G$ in the leading term. |
| 27 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:104` | The block index is held by $n_f=\lceil\log_2(k+1)\rceil$ qubits, so the state is padded to $2^{n_f}$ blocks, each block $b>k$ pinned to zero by its own row $\fhat{b}=0$. |
| 28 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:112` | The mass form keeps the standard form's state, initial state and readout, so it adds no qubits and has no derivative block to rescale (\Cref{sec:descriptor-rescaling}). |
| 29 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:194` | The affine part is $n+1$ Pauli-$Z$ terms (\Cref{eq:lambda-lcu}) and each exception one reflection $Q_m=I-2\ket m\bra m$ (\Cref{lem:affine-band}), so without a source $\Adesc$ is one flat \gls{lcu} of $\mathcal{O}(n)$ terms, \begin{equation} \Adesc = \sum_{t=1}^{L} c_t\,U_t, \qquad U_t = P_t \otimes \mathrm{SHIFT}^{\ell_t}\,D_t, \qquad \alpha_A = \sum_{t=1}^{L} \|c_t\|, \label{eq:descriptor-lcu} \end{equation} where the block word $P_t$, a Pauli word $X^xZ^z$ on the block register or $X^x\Pi_c$, with $\Pi_c$ realised by a flag qubit, for a term that reads block $c$ alone, places the term's row and column blocks, $\mathrm{SHIFT}^{\ell_t}$ is the truncated shift by $\ell_t$, and $D_t$ is the identity, a $Z_j$ or a reflection. |
| 30 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:203` | The truncated shift is not unitary on the axis register's $n$ qubits; it is applied as a cyclic addition on that register widened by one overflow qubit, where rows falling outside the interval land in the half that the final projection discards, so that qubit is the shift's only ancilla. |
| 31 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:216` | The compiled circuit for R2 (second order, no source) on one axis of $n$ qubits, each register labelled with its width. |
| 32 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:240` | The ancilla are the selector registers, the overflow qubit and a flag, and only the diagonal selector's width depends on $n$: for R2 it indexes the identity, the $n$ gates $Z_j$ and three reflections, $\lceil\log_2(n+4)\rceil$ qubits. |
| 33 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:242` | The count is of the least order among exact \gls{lcu} encodings that expand their diagonals in Pauli strings, since $\Lambda$ alone has $n+1$ non-zero Walsh coefficients and a combination of $L$ distinct operations needs $\lceil\log_2L\rceil$ selector qubits \cite{chakraborty2025qsvt}; it is not claimed against encodings built otherwise, by arithmetic on the index for instance. |
| 34 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:242` | The structured encoding of the standard form needs $n-1$ selector qubits for $\G$ alone, because it writes $\Uodd$ as one combination of its $N/2$ shifts (\Cref{sec:standard-cost}), so its ancilla grow as $n$ where the descriptor's grow as $\log n$, though an encoding of $\Uodd$ built otherwise is not excluded. |
| 35 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:244` | It approaches one on regular problems, $\alpha_R/\\|R_{\mathrm{desc}}\\|_2=\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{alpha_ratio}$ on R2a at $n=8$. |
| 36 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:244` | It stays near two on singular ones, $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R3a/descriptor/n7/T3/filter=minimax}{alpha_ratio}$ on R3a at $n=7$, because $\zeta\Dt$, $\zeta^2\Bt$ and $\zeta^2a_2$ are then comparable and comparable terms add in $\ell^1$ even when each is tight. |
| 37 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:244` | On two axes it is $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R5b/descriptor/n4x4/T3/filter=minimax}{alpha_ratio}$ on R5b at $n=4$ per axis. |
| 38 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:244` | In the terms of \Cref{sec:standard}, the encoder's cost $\alpha_H/\\|H\\|$ on R2a at $n=8$ is then $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{hamiltonian_ratio}$ for the descriptor against $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{hamiltonian_ratio}$ for the structured standard form, which is why an as-built comparison favours the descriptor (\Cref{sec:pipeline-design}). |

## Filter ratio (1.67–1.92×, 1.75–1.82×)

| # | Location | Sentence |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:17` | Every term of their Hamiltonian has an efficient circuit, but how many applications a preparation needs, a number that grows with the square root of the Hamiltonian's norm over its spectral gap, was left open. |
| 2 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:19` | For a critically damped oscillator on eight qubits, an exact encoding of the differentiation matrix lowers the estimated number of applications from $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax/encoder=published}{degree}$ with the published encoding to $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$, and no encoding of the same Hamiltonian can bring it below $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{ideal_degree}$. |
| 3 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:21` | For linear problems on one axis with polynomial coefficients, analytic data and a leading coefficient that never vanishes, we prove that the spectral gap stays bounded away from zero as the resolution grows, so the number of applications grows about linearly in $N$; for the oscillator it falls to $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$, although on constant-coefficient problems each application can cost more gates. |
| 4 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:25` | Circuits of up to $24$ qubits are verified against the operators they encode, and simulated circuits of up to $\res{stateprep/R4d/descriptor/n3/T1/filter=minimax}{n_qubits}$ qubits agree with an exact emulation to within $10^{-10}$. |
| 5 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:85` | Every route pays a ratio that the discretisation fixes before any circuit is built: the condition number of a linear system, the strength of a nonlinearity against the dissipation in a linearised equation, or the norm of an encoded Hamiltonian against its spectral gap. |
| 6 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | Take the critically damped oscillator $f''+4f'+4f=0$, one of Wu et al.'s examples and here called R2a, at $n=8$ qubits, with the minimax edge filter, the polynomial of least degree that suppresses the excited states to $\varepsilon=\res[1,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{epsilon}$. |
| 7 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | With Wu et al.'s encoding of the differentiation matrix the filter's degree is $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax/encoder=published}{degree}$. |
| 8 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | An exact encoding of the same Hamiltonian brings it down to $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$, and no encoding of that Hamiltonian can do better than $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{ideal_degree}$, the degree at the ideal-encoding bound $\alpha=\\|R\\|$, where $R$ is the residual operator whose Gram matrix is $H$. |
| 9 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:91` | The descriptor form developed in this thesis is such a Hamiltonian, and on the same problem its filter needs a degree of $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$ as built and $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{ideal_degree}$ at its own ideal bound. |
| 10 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:175` | Their partial differential and nonlinear examples do not reproduce, and the hypotheses tested are recorded (\Cref{sec:standard}, \Cref{app:reproduction}). \item An exact factorisation of the differentiation matrix gives an encoding of $\G$ with $\mathcal{O}(n^2)$ gates and $n+4$ ancilla qubits, within a factor $\res[3]{derivative/n10}{structured_ratio}$ of its norm at $n=10$, against $\res[2,sci]{derivative/n10}{published_ratio}$ for Wu et al.'s approximate encoding. |
| 11 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:177` | A trusted kernel, one-dimensional and well isolated, turns out not to certify that the lift has converged (\Cref{sec:extensions,sec:results-nonlinear,sec:results-ns}). \item One preparation pipeline serves both forms, so that a difference measured between them is a difference between their residuals. |
| 12 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:178` | Every Hamiltonian of at most $24$ qubits is verified whole against the operator assembled from the problem, every lift and coupling term by term at any size, and wherever both run, the simulated circuit equals the exact emulation within $10^{-10}$ before normalising on every run of the minimax filter (\Cref{sec:pipeline,sec:results-faithful}). \end{enumerate} |
| 13 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:181` | The evidence comes in three tiers, named wherever a number is quoted: circuit simulation (T1), which reaches $\res{stateprep/R4d/descriptor/n3/T1/filter=minimax}{n_qubits}$ qubits; exact emulation (T2), provably equal to the circuit's post-selected output, which reaches filter degrees of $2^{20}$; and resource estimates (T3) beyond that, a circuit too wide to simulate being reported as beyond the simulation budget and never as out of the algorithm's reach. |
| 14 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:181` | Neither form is polylogarithmic in $N$, and no advantage over classical solvers is claimed: on one axis a classical spectral solver needs only $\mathcal{O}(N)$ operations \cite{olver2013}. |
| 15 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:181` | The floor on the standard form's degree is shown for polynomial filters of the walk, and since Lin and Tong's optimality result for ground-state preparation assumes access to $H$ rather than to its residual \cite{lintong2020groundstate}, whether it binds every algorithm is left open. |
| 16 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:85` | We call the ratio $\alpha_H/\\|H\\|\ge1$ the \emph{encoder's cost} and $\\|H\\|$ itself the \emph{Hamiltonian's cost}: the first belongs to the circuit, the second to the operator. |
| 17 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:216` | We call it \emph{trusted} when the residual's near-kernel is one-dimensional, its smallest singular value separated from the next by a ratio of at least $20$ (\Cref{sec:pipeline-reference}). |
| 18 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:270` | Evidence for every row: \Cref{app:reproduction}.} \label{tab:deviations} \footnotesize \begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.14\textwidth} p{0.34\textwidth} p{0.36\textwidth} p{0.10\textwidth}@{}} \toprule Choice & This thesis & Defect of the printed choice & Where \\ \midrule \multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\textit{Data: what the residual carries}} \\ \addlinespace[2pt] Constraint points & the exact point, set up from the reference solution & a rounded zero the solution lacks floors the error & \Cref{sec:standard-constraints} \\ Sources & Chebyshev coefficients on the lifted register & a truncated Maclaurin series poses a different equation & \Cref{sec:standard-operators} \\ PDE data & datum slices for initial and boundary functions; the heat data at $t=-1$ & hand-picked invariant sets let the near-kernel grow with $n$; data at $t=0$ pose a backward heat problem & \Cref{sec:standard-constraints} \\ Double zero & R3c and R3d solved for $v=f/\sqrt{1-x^2}$ & a branch point limits convergence to algebraic & \Cref{sec:standard-constraints} \\ \midrule \multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\textit{Reach: what the residual can express}} \\ \addlinespace[2pt] Axes & any number (two run) & a limit of scope: two axes & \Cref{app:pihm-multi-axis} \\ Fields & several fields on one state, ratio rows, realification & a limit of scope: one real scalar field & \Cref{sec:extensions} \\ Nonlinearity & the Carleman lift, in both forms; the doubled space kept in the standard form & the doubled space projects onto a kernel of dimension at least $2^{2n-1}$ & \Cref{sec:doubled-space,sec:carleman} \\ \midrule \multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\textit{Pipeline: how the residual is encoded, filtered, solved and read}} \\ \addlinespace[2pt] Hamiltonian & the stacked residual, solved and encoded from $R_{\mathrm{std}}$; $\Hstd$ never formed & forming $H$ squares the residual's conditioning & \Cref{sec:standard-form,sec:pipeline-reference} \\ Encoding of $\G$ & an exact factorisation, $\alpha$ within a factor $2.12$ of $\\|\G\\|_2$ & an approximate encoding, $\alpha$ looser than $\\|\G\\|_2$ by about $N^3$ & \Cref{sec:standard-factorisation} \\ Filter & the minimax edge filter, its degree set a priori & the time rule does not reach the suppression $\varepsilon$ & \Cref{sec:pipeline-filter} \\ Phases and scale & \gls{gqsp} phases from the target alone; scaled by $\alpha_R^2$ & fitted phases carry no guarantee; $\\|H\\|_F$ is not a subnormalisation & \Cref{sec:pipeline-walk,sec:pipeline-filter} \\ Initial state & the geometric product state; its overlap $\gamma$ reported & not specified & \Cref{sec:pipeline-filter} \\ Readout & the field decoded classically from the prepared state & the interferometric identity assumes $p_G=1$ & \Cref{sec:pipeline-readout} \\ \bottomrule \end{tabular} \end{table} |
| 19 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:328` | The claim that decides its cost is the prefactor of \Cref{eq:pihm-published-prefactor}, which is valid but not compared with $\\|\G\\|_2$: it grows as $N^5/2$ against $\\|\G\\|_2\sim N^2/(2j_{-3/4,1})$, so its looseness tends to $j_{-3/4,1}N^3$, and at $n=7$ it is \res[2,sci]{derivative/n7}{published_ratio} (exact classical computation, \Cref{app:alpha-G}). |
| 20 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:410` | At the sizes reached the ratio $\alpha_{\G}/\\|\G\\|_2$ rises from $\res[3]{derivative/n2}{structured_ratio}$ at $n=2$ to $\res[3]{derivative/n10}{structured_ratio}$ at $n=10$ (\Cref{tab:alpha-G-full}), against a ratio for Wu et al.'s encoding that grows as $N^3$. |
| 21 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:438` | On R2a at $n=8$ the stacked residual's encoding has $\alpha_R=\res[3,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{alpha_R}$ against $\\|R_{\mathrm{std}}\\|_2=\res[3,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{norm_R}$, both computed exactly from the built circuit and the assembled residual, a ratio of $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{alpha_ratio}$, levelling off towards the constant looseness of the residual's dominant term $\G^2$ (\Cref{app:G-limits}), though no limit is proved for the whole residual. |
| 22 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:438` | Since the degree is linear in $\alpha_R$, this ratio is the whole of the encoder's cost in the degree; in $\alpha_H=\alpha_R^2$ it is squared, $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{hamiltonian_ratio}$ at $n=8$. |
| 23 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:438` | With Wu et al.'s encoding the same ratio is $\res[2,sci]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/standard/n7/T3/filter=minimax/encoder=published}{alpha_ratio}$ at $n=7$ and $\res[2,sci]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax/encoder=published}{alpha_ratio}$ at $n=8$: it grows as $N^{3k}$, a factor $N^3$ for each of the $k$ factors of $\G$ in the leading term. |
| 24 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:440` | On the coefficient vectors of the linear polynomials, $\G$ and the lifts act exactly and every row evaluates the function, so by Parseval's identity (\Cref{eq:eta-e}) the quotient $\\|R_{\mathrm{std}}b\\|^2/\\|b\\|^2$ on them is a ratio of integrals and point values that does not depend on $N$; by Courant--Fischer, $\Delta\le\lambda_1(\Hstd)\le C$, with $C$ fixed by the equation and its data, for any polynomial coefficients (\Cref{prop:std-gap-bound}). |
| 25 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:440` | Because no block encoding has $\alpha_R$ below $\\|R_{\mathrm{std}}\\|_2$, the minimax edge filter's degree (\Cref{eq:minimax-degree}) then satisfies \begin{equation} \begin{gathered} \\|\Hstd\\| = \Theta\bigl(N^{4k}\bigr), \quad \Delta \le C \quad\Longrightarrow\quad d = \Omega\bigl(N^{2k}\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr)\ \text{for every block encoding of } R_{\mathrm{std}}, \\ \text{(order } k\text{, constant coefficients, } a_k\ne0\text{, constraints of order below } k\text{)}, \end{gathered} \label{eq:standard-wall} \end{equation} and $d=\Theta(N^{2k}\log(1/\varepsilon))$ for the structured encoding wherever the gap is also bounded below and $\alpha_R/\\|R_{\mathrm{std}}\\|_2$ stays bounded, as both are on R2a. |
| 26 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:453` | Every block encoding of $R_{\mathrm{std}}$ has $\alpha_R\ge\\|R_{\mathrm{std}}\\|_2$, and the minimax filter's degree does not increase as $\alpha_R$ falls, so the degree at $\alpha_R=\\|R_{\mathrm{std}}\\|_2$, which we call the \emph{ideal-encoding bound}, is the least this filter needs on this residual through any encoding of it (\Cref{prop:ideal-degree}). |
| 27 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:169` | One weight per block is what keeps that factor bounded; a per-amplitude equilibration \cite{vandersluis1969} would need a diagonal block encoding of its own, at $\Theta(\dim w)$ gates, and no circuit here uses one. |
| 28 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:220` | The selectors are factored, one for the block word and, on each axis, one for the diagonal and one for the shift, each only as wide as the distinct values its terms use, so SELECT applies each distinct operation once rather than once per term and the ancilla stay $\mathcal{O}(\log n)$ (\Cref{sec:descriptor-cost}). |
| 29 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:240` | The gate count follows because each distinct operation is applied once and the shift's multi-controlled ladder costs $\mathcal{O}(n^2)$; a source's rotation tree loads its $N$ coefficients with $\mathcal{O}(N)$ gates, as for any general state, and with $\mathrm{poly}(n)$ when only a fixed number of them is non-zero. |
| 30 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:242` | The count is of the least order among exact \gls{lcu} encodings that expand their diagonals in Pauli strings, since $\Lambda$ alone has $n+1$ non-zero Walsh coefficients and a combination of $L$ distinct operations needs $\lceil\log_2L\rceil$ selector qubits \cite{chakraborty2025qsvt}; it is not claimed against encodings built otherwise, by arithmetic on the index for instance. |
| 31 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:244` | The proposition concerns the system rows; for the whole stacked residual, constraints included, the ratio is computed exactly from the built circuit's $\alpha_R$ and the assembled residual's norm. |
| 32 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:244` | It approaches one on regular problems, $\alpha_R/\\|R_{\mathrm{desc}}\\|_2=\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{alpha_ratio}$ on R2a at $n=8$. |
| 33 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:244` | It stays near two on singular ones, $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R3a/descriptor/n7/T3/filter=minimax}{alpha_ratio}$ on R3a at $n=7$, because $\zeta\Dt$, $\zeta^2\Bt$ and $\zeta^2a_2$ are then comparable and comparable terms add in $\ell^1$ even when each is tight. |
| 34 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:244` | On two axes it is $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R5b/descriptor/n4x4/T3/filter=minimax}{alpha_ratio}$ on R5b at $n=4$ per axis. |
| 35 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:244` | In the terms of \Cref{sec:standard}, the encoder's cost $\alpha_H/\\|H\\|$ on R2a at $n=8$ is then $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{hamiltonian_ratio}$ for the descriptor against $\res[3]{looseness/stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{hamiltonian_ratio}$ for the structured standard form, which is why an as-built comparison favours the descriptor (\Cref{sec:pipeline-design}). |

gap proved $\Theta(1)$ & the chain with the a priori $\zeta$ for $k\ge2$, the mass form for $k=1$; expect more gates per query at small $n$ \\ Singular, simple zero (R3, R4a) & degree about $\tilde\Theta(N^2)$; against $N^4$ where the zero is at one end (R4a), none at the ideal bound where it is at both (R3) & $\zeta=N/2$; judge at the ideal bound, since as built the gain on R3 is the encoder's \\ Singular, double zero & none claimed: balancing needs a norm above the standard form's own & substitute $f=\sqrt{1-x^2}\,v$ to leave simple zeros, as for R3c and R3d \\ Two axes, no mixed derivative (R5) & degree's exponent per axis below the standard form's; reversed on the heat equation at $n=4$ per axis; gap falls even when rescaled & $\zeta_a=N_a$; budget a state of four to eight blocks; compare at the ideal bound and expect no guaranteed gain \\ First-order systems (R7) & as on a regular problem: about $N$ against $N^2$ & the mass form, with no block register and a larger gap than a chain's; data as ratio rows; the coupling as matchings \\ Complex-valued linear equations & that of the real system after realification (R7 is $z'=-\mathrm{i}\pi z$ realified) & realify, at one more field-register qubit; native complex preparation is not built \\ Polynomial nonlinearity, lift converging (R8; R9 to $a/\nu=3$; R10 to $a/\nu=10$, and at $30$ on $12$ modes) & through the time axis only, from $n_t=4$, narrowing as the modes grow & the Carleman lift, mass form in time; raise $n_t$ with the truncation; measure the lift's error per order; never the doubled space, which projects \\ Past the lift's boundary (R9 at $a/\nu=10$; R10 at $a/\nu=30$ from $28$ modes) & none: the kernel stays trusted while the lift diverges & shorten the horizon or lower the strength; read convergence from the lift's error, never from trust \\ \bottomrule \end{tabularx} \end{table} |

| 98 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:145` | The widths stay modest, the widest circuits estimated, Navier--Stokes on twelve modes at $K=4$ and $n_t=5$, needing $\res{stateprep/R10c-M12/descriptor/n5/T3/carleman=4/filter=minimax}{n_qubits}$ qubits in the descriptor and $\res{stateprep/R10c-M12/standard/n5/T3/carleman=4/filter=minimax}{n_qubits}$ in the standard form; the obstacle is the gate count. |
| 99 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:145` | The descriptor's count on R2a buys one second-order equation on $N=2^8$ coefficients, a problem of no classical difficulty, and the reformulation divides the count by about the ratio of the two degrees, which grows as $N^{2k-1}$ on such a problem; that alone does not make the problem worth a quantum computer. |
| 100 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:145` | A filter of $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$ sequential walks of $\res{query/stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{gates}$ two-qubit gates each lies far beyond the depth that uncorrected processors execute reliably \cite{preskill2018nisq}, which is why noisy hardware is out of scope. |
| 101 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:167` | Beyond the $\res{stateprep/R4d/descriptor/n3/T1/filter=minimax}{n_qubits}$ qubits of circuit simulation, exact emulation stands in for the circuit by the argument of \Cref{sec:pipeline-verification}, which the agreement of the two confirms only where both run; the widest reflections, of the two-axis descriptor problems and the larger lifts, are built and counted but not verified whole, and Navier--Stokes is reached by emulation and estimate alone (\Cref{sec:results-ns}). |
| 102 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:173` | A trusted kernel certifies the lifted linear problem and not the lift, so every Carleman preparation needs the lift's measured error beside it, and the thesis has no test of divergence that reads the residual alone (\Cref{sec:results-nonlinear}). |
| 103 | `2_body/10_conclusions.tex:40` | An exact factorisation of $\G$ lowers the estimated degree on the critically damped oscillator R2a at $n=8$ from $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax/encoder=published}{degree}$ to $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$, which is the encoder's share. |
| 104 | `2_body/10_conclusions.tex:42` | On R2a at $n=8$ it is $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$, a figure exact emulation confirms, and $\res{stateprep/R2a/descriptor/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{ideal_degree}$ at the ideal bound, where the standard form needs $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}$ and $\res[2,sci]{stateprep/R2a/standard/n8/T3/filter=minimax}{ideal_degree}$ (resource estimates). |
| 105 | `2_body/10_conclusions.tex:44` | On two axes the rescaled gap still falls: on the heat equation the descriptor's degree grows as $N^{\res[2]{stateprep/R5b/descriptor/growth/T3/filter=minimax}{degree}}$ per axis between four and five qubits per axis, and at four the standard form's is the lower, as built and at the ideal bound. |
| 106 | `2_body/10_conclusions.tex:48` | On a regular problem a preparation from an initial state of overlap $\gamma$ with the solution costs $\tilde\Theta(N\,\mathrm{poly}(n)/\gamma)$ gates in the descriptor form against $\tilde\Theta(N^{2k}\,\mathrm{poly}(n)/\gamma)$ in the standard form, while on one axis a classical spectral solver needs only $\mathcal{O}(N)$ operations. |
| 107 | `2_body/10_conclusions.tex:55` | Hardware is further away than the circuit widths suggest: the smallest preparation, the decay $f'+2f=0$ (R1) at $n=2$ in the descriptor form, a circuit of only $\res{stateprep/R1/descriptor/n2/T1/filter=minimax}{n_qubits}$ qubits, applies $\res{stateprep/R1/descriptor/n2/T1/filter=minimax}{degree}$ queries of $\res{encode/R1/descriptor/n2}{cx_count}$ two-qubit gates each, which would place a first demonstration on early error-corrected machines. |

| 43 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:167` | Beyond the $\res{stateprep/R4d/descriptor/n3/T1/filter=minimax}{n_qubits}$ qubits of circuit simulation, exact emulation stands in for the circuit by the argument of \Cref{sec:pipeline-verification}, which the agreement of the two confirms only where both run; the widest reflections, of the two-axis descriptor problems and the larger lifts, are built and counted but not verified whole, and Navier--Stokes is reached by emulation and estimate alone (\Cref{sec:results-ns}). |
| 44 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:173` | A trusted kernel certifies the lifted linear problem and not the lift, so every Carleman preparation needs the lift's measured error beside it, and the thesis has no test of divergence that reads the residual alone (\Cref{sec:results-nonlinear}). |
| 45 | `2_body/10_conclusions.tex:46` | Wu et al.'s doubled space encodes a quadratic term exactly, but its kernel has dimension $\res{build/R6a/standard/n5}{kernel_count}$ in a space of $\res{build/R6a/standard/n5}{dimension}$ at $n=5$, so a filter returns a projection onto it. |

| 47 | `2_body/10_conclusions.tex:46` | On Burgers' equation at strength $a/\nu=10$, and on Navier--Stokes at $a/\nu=30$ from $28$ modes, the lift diverges while its kernel stays trusted, so the filter faithfully prepares a linear problem that is no longer the nonlinear one. |

## Complexity bounds, poly-log, speed-up (general)

| # | Location | Sentence |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:17` | A register of $n$ qubits holds $N=2^n$ amplitudes, so a quantum algorithm can store a discretised solution in exponentially few qubits. |
| 2 | `1_header/5_abstract.tex:25` | Neither formulation offers an exponential speed-up, and no advantage over classical solvers is claimed. |
| 3 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:85` | A register of $n$ qubits holds a vector of $N=2^n$ amplitudes, and this exponential compression is the reason quantum algorithms for differential equations are proposed \cite{berry2014highorder,childsliu2020spectral,liu2021carleman}. |
| 4 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:89` | That number is the degree of the filter, and for a block encoding of $H$ at subnormalisation $\alpha_H\ge\\|H\\|$, a gap $\Delta$ above the ground state and a suppression $\varepsilon$ of the excited states, it grows as $d=\Theta\bigl(\sqrt{\alpha_H/\Delta}\,\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr)$ (\Cref{eq:degree}). |
| 5 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:181` | Neither form is polylogarithmic in $N$, and no advantage over classical solvers is claimed: on one axis a classical spectral solver needs only $\mathcal{O}(N)$ operations \cite{olver2013}. |
| 6 | `2_body/1_introduction.tex:181` | The floor on the standard form's degree is shown for polynomial filters of the walk, and since Lin and Tong's optimality result for ground-state preparation assumes access to $H$ rather than to its residual \cite{lintong2020groundstate}, whether it binds every algorithm is left open. |
| 7 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:440` | Because no block encoding has $\alpha_R$ below $\\|R_{\mathrm{std}}\\|_2$, the minimax edge filter's degree (\Cref{eq:minimax-degree}) then satisfies \begin{equation} \begin{gathered} \\|\Hstd\\| = \Theta\bigl(N^{4k}\bigr), \quad \Delta \le C \quad\Longrightarrow\quad d = \Omega\bigl(N^{2k}\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr)\ \text{for every block encoding of } R_{\mathrm{std}}, \\ \text{(order } k\text{, constant coefficients, } a_k\ne0\text{, constraints of order below } k\text{)}, \end{gathered} \label{eq:standard-wall} \end{equation} and $d=\Theta(N^{2k}\log(1/\varepsilon))$ for the structured encoding wherever the gap is also bounded below and $\alpha_R/\\|R_{\mathrm{std}}\\|_2$ stays bounded, as both are on R2a. |
| 8 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:453` | At second order, the order of Wu et al.'s examples, $\\|\Hstd\\|=\Theta(N^8)$ and no block encoding brings the degree below order $N^4\log(1/\varepsilon)$. |
| 9 | `2_body/4_standard.tex:459` | The Hamiltonian's cost, $\Omega(N^{2k}\log(1/\varepsilon))$ queries, remains under every encoding, a factor $N^2$ for each derivative eliminated through $\G$, and a gap bounded independently of $N$ cannot offset it. |
| 10 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:260` | Neither form is polylogarithmic in $N$: $\tilde\Theta(N)$ is exponential in $n$, and the claim is a lower exponent only. |
| 11 | `2_body/5_descriptor.tex:289` | Neither is polylogarithmic. \item \emph{The limit. |
| 12 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:68` | Neither form is polylogarithmic in $N$. |
| 13 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:68` | The degree of \Cref{eq:degree}, $d=\Theta\bigl(\sqrt{\alpha_H/\Delta}\,\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr)$ with $\alpha_H\ge\\|H\\|$ for any block encoding, is the residual's effective condition number times $\log(1/\varepsilon)$. |
| 14 | `2_body/9_discussion.tex:70` | Lin and Tong prove, by reduction from unstructured search, that their ground-state algorithm's dependence on the gap and the overlap is essentially optimal for an oracle that block-encodes $H$ \cite{lintong2020groundstate}. |

reversed on the heat equation at $n=4$ per axis; gap falls even when rescaled & $\zeta_a=N_a$; budget a state of four to eight blocks; compare at the ideal bound and expect no guaranteed gain \\ First-order systems (R7) & as on a regular problem: about $N$ against $N^2$ & the mass form, with no block register and a larger gap than a chain's; data as ratio rows; the coupling as matchings \\ Complex-valued linear equations & that of the real system after realification (R7 is $z'=-\mathrm{i}\pi z$ realified) & realify, at one more field-register qubit; native complex preparation is not built \\ Polynomial nonlinearity, lift converging (R8; R9 to $a/\nu=3$; R10 to $a/\nu=10$, and at $30$ on $12$ modes) & through the time axis only, from $n_t=4$, narrowing as the modes grow & the Carleman lift, mass form in time; raise $n_t$ with the truncation; measure the lift's error per order; never the doubled space, which projects \\ Past the lift's boundary (R9 at $a/\nu=10$; R10 at $a/\nu=30$ from $28$ modes) & none: the kernel stays trusted while the lift diverges & shorten the horizon or lower the strength; read convergence from the lift's error, never from trust \\ \bottomrule \end{tabularx} \end{table} |
